## 6. Interaction design — five unsurfaced reads, and one surface that was MISSING

Each is deliberately unsurfaced **with its reason recorded**, and each needs a
design decision rather than wiring. Verified 2026-10-22 against `mobile-ui/src`:
all four return zero hits, while `get_discourse_health` returns three — the
read is wired, the publish is not. **AND A SIXTH ROW IS NOT A READ AT ALL:
6.7, opened and BUILT 2026-10-27**, was a missing WRITE —
`publish_constitution` was reachable only by founding a domain, so a membrane
could demand promises a joiner had no way to make. That one was a gap rather
than a decision, which is why it was built the same day it was found. **A
FIFTH JOINED ON 2026-10-26 NIGHT AND IT
WAS A DIFFERENT ANIMAL: 6.6 was unsurfaced by oversight rather than by
decision**, needed no design question answered first, and **is now the only one
of the five that is BUILT** — `EpistemicAgent.promiseCompliance()` and
`scripts/live-verify/promise-compliance.mjs`, 19 checks on a real conductor.
So the four that remain are the four that were always a decision.
**AND THEN THERE WERE THREE, 2026-11-02: 6.1 IS BUILT.** It was always the
oddest of the four, because by 2026-10-28 its design question had been answered
and demonstrated — the honest rendering of an absent cone — leaving only the
application of that answer to `critiques_unseen_by`'s own output, which is
wiring. The three that remain (6.2, 6.4, 6.5) still hold real decisions.
**AND A TENTH ROW IS NEITHER A READ NOR A WRITE: 6.10, opened 2026-10-29.** It
is the compose-time moment itself — the only point at which a permanent entry is
still free to change — and it is the first row in this file that would put a
language model in the app. It is `[D]` for exactly the reason the other four
are: *what must be on screen before a user signs?* What it does **not** share
with them is a protocol dependency, because it writes nothing.

**AND TWO ROWS WERE OPENED BY A USER RATHER THAN BY A READING: 6.12 AND 6.13,
2026-11-09.** Every other row in this section was found by looking at the code
and asking what was unsurfaced. These two began with a question a pilot
participant asked out loud the day they published — *how do you search for what
others wrote?* — and the answer is that they cannot, unless somebody tells them
the exact domain string first. **6.12 is the finding and 6.13 is what the
attempt to verify it ran into.** 6.12 splits the way none of the others do: a
small `[M]` half plainly missing (a claim did not display its own domain) and
a `[D]` half the project's own security reasoning has already half-answered
against. 6.13 was not a decision at all — the Browse tab could not tell an
empty domain from an unloaded one or from a failed read, while By Author
distinguished all three a thousand lines earlier in the same file.

**BOTH OF THOSE HALVES WERE BUILT 2026-11-10, AND THE `[D]` IS EXACTLY WHERE IT
WAS.** 6.13 is closed and gated; 6.12's breadcrumb is on every claim card. What
neither touches is the question a reader who has arrived nowhere asks, which is
the half that needs a decision rather than a morning — so the useful reading of
this pair is that **the user's question split into two hours of work and one
open question, and nothing about doing the work moved the question.**

**AND THREE MORE CAME FROM ONE REPLY, 2026-11-11: 6.18, 6.19 AND 6.20.** The
same participant who opened 6.17 came back the following day with three
sentences about the screen they had just used — who is speaking, how big the
box is, and how the text is shaped — and all three were real. **The ratio in
this section is now the thing to notice.** Every row opened by reading the
code needed a design decision before anything could be built; of the six
opened by a user, five were plainly missing and four were built the same day
they arrived. A non-technical reader describing what they cannot do has turned
out to be the highest-yield source of work in this file, and the reason is not
that the code was carelessly written — it is that the person who wrote it
cannot see the screen for the first time twice.

**AND THE REPLY CLOSED A QUESTION OF OURS, WHICH MATTERS BECAUSE THE RULE HERE
IS NEVER TO RE-ASK.** They wrote *"I think i misread what you wrote, it will
reach in next build ok"*, so the *"still not showing the record"* half of their
earlier report is 6.17 waiting on a release rather than a second defect. The
*"unable to see your full critique"* half reads, in the light of this message,
as 6.18 and 6.20 rather than as truncation — they describe the text as all
there and the problem as unreadable — but **that is an inference and is
recorded as one.** Nothing is asked again.

**AND A THIRD REPLY, 2026-11-12, ANSWERED A DECISION THAT WAS BEING HELD FOR
THE HUMAN — THE FIRST TIME THAT HAS HAPPENED IN THIS FILE.** 6.19's `[D]` was
*where a reply appears*: the critique list arrives in the order the DHT returned
it, a nested reply has no place in that order, and indenting one is a client
deciding a thread's shape. Unprompted, and without having been shown the row,
the participant described the shape they wanted: *"the reply comes under a
critique, in where others agreeing or disagreeing with that particular critique
to the claim can reply. A new critique gets its own section. It provides
organisation."* That is an answer to the question as the row framed it, given by
the person the row is owed to, and it is taken as one — see 6.19, where the
`[D]` is closed and the `[M]` is unblocked. **A decision this file was holding
because it was not ours to make turned out to be answerable by asking nobody:
they volunteered it.** The same message asked for one thing more, filed
separately because it is independently shippable: *"I'd include an option if
possible in where the list minimizes after 3-4 critiques but can be opened to
see all, to not clutter?"* — 6.22.

**AND ONE ROW IN THIS SECTION WAS NOT REPORTED BY ANYBODY — 6.24, FOUND BY A
HARNESS THAT COULD NOT SEE WHAT IT HAD JUST PUBLISHED.** Building 6.22's gate
meant publishing three more critiques and reading them back; collapsing the
panel and re-opening it showed the old three, and only a full page reload
showed six. **The critique panel is a snapshot taken the first time it was
opened.** Its cause is one guarded line whose own neighbouring comment says
the skip-on-re-open was understood — for the number beside the list, which is
refreshed, and not for the list. **It is §4.4's failure mode reached from a
direction §4.4 does not discuss:** that section blames per-user adaptation for
two readers seeing different artifacts, and a session cache produces exactly
that with no adaptation anywhere. **6.22's fold was measured against the rule
and cleared it; the cache beside it has been breaking it since the panel was
written.**

**AND A FOURTH REPLY THE SAME DAY CONFIRMED TWO FIXES AND REOPENED A THIRD
PROBLEM UNDER A NEW READING — 6.23.** The confirmations first, because they are
the only direct evidence this file has that anything shipped works for the
person it was shipped for: *"great to see the domains that a user has published
in. Makes things a lot easier"* is 6.16 landing, and *"thats a good one"* is the
reply layout. **The reopening is the one that matters.** On a build that
already carries 6.18's byline they wrote *"People writing critiques, still
unclear if it is one person or the same."* 6.18 read their first report as
*there is no byline*; the half it never answered was the next sentence — *"you
wouldnt know if you're speaking to the same or others"* — which is not about
presence but about whether twelve base64 characters are something a human can
compare. **A screen can pass every structural assertion in `reading-a-thread.mjs`
and still not answer the reader's question**, and the gap between *the data is
on screen* and *the screen answers the question* is where this pilot keeps
finding work. They also answered the one question we asked: **macOS**, which
settles 6.21's platform without a log — the log being the thing that did not
exist.

**AND THE SAME MESSAGE OPENED 6.21, WHICH IS THE WORST DEFECT THIS PILOT HAS
TURNED UP AND IS NOT IN THE HAPP AT ALL.** *"App shows a window stating new
version available. Install and restart. Clicking this - does nothing. No update
happens."* The release that was cut because this participant asked for it could
not be delivered by the mechanism built to deliver it, and the way it failed
left no record of itself on their machine or in this repository.

| # | item | who | blocked on |
|---|---|---|---|
| 6.1 | ~~`critiques_unseen_by` has no screen~~ **CLOSED 2026-11-02 — it has one, and the row's own remaining half is what closed it.** Its HONESTY RULE was built and checked on 2026-10-28, and the row was narrower than it was. The rule: a screen must render `cone_recorded` as prominently as `unseen`, or silence reads as ignorance. The 2026-10-26 reclassification was right: `cone_recorded` was false for every critique in existence because no client populated `past_cone`, so the screen could render **one state and only one**, and a one-state indicator asserts nothing. 6.5's agent half unblocked it — an SDK agent now records a cone that is true by construction — so both states occur and the rule is enforceable. **BUILT:** `renderConeState` draws the state on every critique and every position, at the SAME typographic weight in both cases (a fainter absent case would re-assert ignorance typographically after the wording had avoided it), and in a list where one entry says what it saw, an unmarked neighbour reads as the weaker claim by implication — so every row states which it is. **VERIFIED:** `positions-ui.mjs` §4, five checks, both states on one screen. **AND IT CAUGHT THE ROW'S OWN DEFECT BEING COMMITTED BY ITS OWN FIX:** the first version distinguished an *absent* `past_cone` from an explicit `[]` and rendered the latter as *"Recorded having seen none of this claim's other objections"* — **a positive claim of having seen nothing, which is exactly what this row exists to prevent.** It is also unrepresentable: `past_cone` is a `Vec<ActionHash>` with `#[serde(default)]`, so an omitted field and `[]` are the SAME STORED VALUE, and the coordinator's own `cone_recorded = !past_cone.is_empty()` makes both *not recorded*. **There are two states, not three**, and the harness surfaced it by failing against correct behaviour. **WHAT IS STILL OPEN:** `critiques_unseen_by` itself has no screen — the cone is rendered from the entry, not from that read, so the `unseen` set and its `total_known` caveat (another observer may know more) are still unsurfaced. That is a smaller row than this one was, and it is no longer blocked on 6.5 or on a design decision — the honest rendering is now demonstrated, so the remaining work is to apply it to that read's own output. **AND THAT IS WHAT WAS DONE, 2026-11-02 — `renderUnseenState` plus `loadUnseen`, `positions-ui.mjs` §5/§5b/§5c, 19 checks, 42 in the file, green on a live conductor and watched failing five ways.** **WHAT THE QUERY ADDS THAT THE ENTRY CANNOT, which is why this half was the half left:** §4's line reads the ENTRY, so it states a numerator with its denominator hidden — *"recorded having seen 1 other objection"* says nothing until a reader knows whether there was one other objection or forty, and **no entry can carry that figure**, because it is a count of what THIS conductor resolves over its own arc at read time. So every rendered figure is attributed to *this reader* rather than to the world, which is `total_known` said out loud. **THE DESIGN DECISION INSIDE THE WIRING, and it is an asymmetry that looks at first like a lapse from this row's own rule:** the unseen SET is offered only where a cone was actually recorded. The equal-weight rule governs the STATE — both branches state themselves, same class, same measured weight — but a SET is a different object. Where a cone exists, the omitted siblings are a checkable gap in a statement its author chose to make. Where none exists there is no statement to have a gap in, and the "set" is every objection already on screen above it, so a disclosure there would **manufacture a gap out of a missing feature** and caption the author's neighbours as what they failed to see. The not-recorded branch says why there is nothing to show instead. **AND THE CLIENT STATES THE FIGURES WHILE THE PROTOCOL STATES WHAT THEY SUPPORT:** `UnseenReport.note` is rendered VERBATIM rather than paraphrased, because a paraphrase is a second place for SPEC's own wording to rot — 1.20's bug class, avoided by not duplicating the claim. **THREE OF THE FIVE INJECTIONS WENT RED WHERE NO WORDING CHECK COULD, which is this row's lasting finding:** fading the not-recorded line to `opacity: 0.55` breaks the rule typographically after the words were careful not to, and is caught only by reading `getComputedStyle` back off the page; paraphrasing the coordinator's note turns three red while *"the note is rendered for every critique"* stays green, because **a count of elements cannot tell whose words are in them**; and moving the denial under the list it qualifies turns one red with both wording checks passing, since the denial is still present and merely too late. Full record: `scripts/live-verify/README.md`, injections CJ-CN. **Next free injection ID: CO.** ~~**WHAT THIS DOES NOT COVER:** `Assessment.past_cone` gets no denominator, because nothing can say how many POSITIONS an assessor's cone was recorded out of~~ **THAT CLAIM WAS WRONG IN TWO WAYS AND IS WITHDRAWN, 2026-11-02 — see 6.11, which built the thing it said could not exist.** **(1) IT IS NOT POSITIONS.** `Assessment.past_cone` holds **CRITIQUES** of the target: `validate_assessment` refuses any member that does not resolve to a `Critique` of the same target. So the denominator is the target's critique count, and *how many of the other assessments did this assessor read* is a question the protocol does not record at all — there is no sibling-assessment cone, by design. Had the claim stood, the obvious next build would have been a query counting the wrong set, and the figure it printed would have looked right. **(2) A CLIENT COULD ALREADY HAVE DERIVED IT** — `get_critiques_for` returns the denominator and the cone is on the entry — so *nothing anywhere can say* was false as stated. What the new query actually adds had to be argued rather than assumed: the gating rule travels with the data, the denominator is counted over the conductor's own arc at read time rather than over whatever a panel happened to fetch, and the protocol states what the figures support instead of leaving a client to invent it. **The pattern is this file's own:** a load-bearing prose claim, written in passing while closing a different row, caught by the next piece of work that touched it rather than by any gate. | ~~**M**~~ **DONE 2026-11-02** | nothing — the read has its screen |
| 6.2 | `publish_discourse_reading` | D | same |
| 6.3 | the settlement screen | D | same |
| 6.4 | the assessment screen — **the READ half shipped 2026-10-28 and this row is NOT closed by it.** Other agents' positions now render on the claim card: stance, their own confidence, their reasons, the assessor named, in the protocol's order, with **nothing counting them** — `positions-ui.mjs`, 23 checks, and the central one is that a deliberate 2-1 split is on screen and no tally appears, because a client-computed tally is a verdict nobody published and Invariant #1 through the back door. That half needed no decision: rendering somebody else's attributed entry is the critique panel's settled pattern. **The read was built because this project created the asymmetry** — `agent-sdk` and `mcp-server` gained assessment publishing the same day (1.19), so an agent could file a position on somebody's claim and the person whose claim it was had no surface that showed it, which is the half-surfaced defect pointed the other way. **WHAT REMAINS IS THE WRITE, and it is still a decision, for the reason README §9 already states:** a stance control commits a user to a permanent attributed position under their own key, and doing it honestly means showing what they are assessing ALONGSIDE the objections already raised — `past_cone` records what they had seen. A one-click Supports/Disputes without that is a vote, which is the one thing this entry type is deliberately not. | **D** for the write half | same — the write screen, not the read |
| 6.5 | **A client populates `past_cone` — which is what 6.1 is actually blocked on.** *Opened 2026-10-26 by the §6 brief.* Nothing sends the field: `grep -rn 'past_cone' mobile-ui/src/*.ts agent-sdk/` is empty, it is `#[serde(default)]`, so every `create_critique` commits an empty cone and `cone_recorded` is false everywhere. **This is not a wiring task either**, which is why it is `[D]` and not `[M]`: populating a cone means a CLIENT ASSERTING WHAT ITS USER READ, which is a promise under that user's key with its own honesty problem — a client that over-claims makes its user appear better informed than they were, and **SPEC §2.4 is explicit that validation bites only on under-claiming**: over-claiming is *"possible and harmless"* **to the protocol**, and is not harmless to the person it is attributed to. Until this is decided, 6.1's screen has nothing to show. | D | a decision about what a client may assert on its user's behalf |
| 6.6 | **Does any member actually promise what their membrane requires? Nothing anywhere answers that, and the data has been published all along.** *Opened 2026-10-26 night by writing 7.17's text — `docs/the-joining-promise.md` §2.* `Membrane.required_promises` is **DHT-enforced to be non-empty** at founding, is exported by the N4L serialiser, and is **compared against nobody's `Constitution` in either zome**. Joining does not check it (`join_membrane` is unrestricted by its own comment; `AgentToMembrane` validation at `dna/integrity/src/lib.rs:2456-2495` enforces a well-formed 36-byte tag, tag-equals-real-author, and a real `Membrane` base — nothing about promises), **and that non-enforcement is correct**: membership is a voluntary promise, so a protocol that refused the join would make the membrane an authority over who may speak. **WHAT IS MISSING IS THE READ, AND IT IS TWO CALLS**: `get_membrane_members(membrane)` and `get_agent_constitution(agent)` both exist and are already network reads, so *which members promised what this membrane asks* is a client-side comparison over published data — **no zome change, no fork, nothing to decide first.** **WHY IT MATTERS NOW RATHER THAN ABSTRACTLY: it is what makes 7.17's joining promise checkable rather than decorative**, and the whole argument for asking an invitee for a `Constitution` instead of nothing is that the agreement goes on the record. **THE HONEST LIMIT, which is also why it must not become a gate**: string equality over author-written phrases catches *promised nothing* and cannot catch *promised and did not mean it*; a member may also promise more than the membrane asks, which is not a violation. **It is the only unblocked `[M]` row that is not research** — 3.2 and 3.8 are questions, this is an hour of client code — and unlike 6.1-6.4 it needs no screen decided first, because a founder reading it once before sending an invitation is already the use. **BUILT 2026-10-26 NIGHT, GREEN ON A REAL CONDUCTOR, AND WATCHED FAILING TWICE.** `EpistemicAgent.promiseCompliance()` (`agent-sdk`) is the comparison; `scripts/live-verify/promise-compliance.mjs`, **19 checks**, drives the SHIPPED SDK rather than a local reimplementation, with **four agents** on one conductor: the founder, one who promised everything plus an extra, one who promised some of it and the rest in another domain, and one who published nothing. Added to `conductor.yml`, so it runs on every pull request that could change the answer. **§5 ASSERTS THE NON-ENFORCEMENT ON PURPOSE** — the agent who promised nothing joins successfully and the READING says so rather than the write refusing, because a harness arguing the opposite would be arguing for a different protocol. **§4 is the check worth having**: the right phrase in the WRONG DOMAIN does not count, since a `Promise` carries a domain and a comparison ignoring it would pass anybody who promised anything anywhere. **NEGATIVE EVIDENCE, MEASURED.** Injection 1, domain ignored: **3 reds** — both of §3's and one of §4's, while *"reported as promised elsewhere"* STAYED GREEN, because reporting a promise as out-of-domain and counting it as in-domain are independent; only the pair catches it, which is why §4 has two checks. Injection 2, `hasConstitution` hardcoded true: **1 red**, which is `mode-and-constitution`'s failure one layer down — a member who published nothing reading as one who published something empty. **AND BUILDING IT TRIPPED A GATE FOR THE WRONG REASON, now fixed: 1.16 — and it found 1.17 on the way.** | ~~M~~ **BUILT** | nothing |
| 6.7 | ~~**The protocol's own entry condition had no surface: a membrane may DEMAND promises and a joiner had nowhere to make them.**~~ **FOUND AND BUILT 2026-10-27 while preparing the pilot founding — and it would have broken 7.17 the day it was adopted.** *Measured, not suspected*: `publish_constitution` was called from **exactly one place** in `mobile-ui` (the founding form), and that form published **one** own-promise from a single-line input while accepting **several** demands of others from a textarea. Two consequences, both silent. **(1) An invitee could not promise at all without founding their own domain** — so §3's ask in `docs/the-invitation.md`, adopted hours earlier, was unexecutable in the shipped app. **(2) A founder requiring four phrases published one**, and was therefore missing three of them in the membrane they themselves founded, visible only in a `promiseCompliance` read nobody would run before inviting — the rehearsal missed it because it called the zome directly, where four promises are trivial. **WHAT WAS BUILT**: the founding form's own-promise field is now a textarea, one per line, mirroring the demands it sits beside; and a standalone **Your promises** section on the Domains tab publishes a `Constitution` for a named domain without founding anything — stating, before the button, that it is permanent, that a later publish supersedes for readers without deleting the earlier, and that **it admits you to nothing, because joining is separate and unrestricted**. It reads back what was published rather than reporting success from the absence of an exception. **`scripts/live-verify/promises-ui.mjs`, 14 checks**, green on a real conductor and **watched failing twice** (the founding form reverted to its first own-promise → 2 reds in §5 with §§1-4 green; the standalone form truncated to one line → 3 reds in §2), wired into `ui.yml`. **AND §3 OF THAT HARNESS FOUND 1.18 ON ITS FIRST RUN.** **This is the only one of §6's rows that was a GAP rather than a decision**, which is why it was built the same day it was found. | ~~M~~ **BUILT**; the release that ships it is `[H]` | nothing — but the UI reaches an invitee only through a desktop build, so 7.4 waits on the `v0.3.1` patch release |

As shipped, the first field of that flow asks for a value the app will not show you and will not let you copy. **WHY 6.7 DID NOT CATCH IT:** 6.7 fixed *promise publishing* having exactly one route and audited that one surface, not the neighbouring flow with the same defect class. **The general form, and the reason this row is worth its length: a form field whose only valid value is an identifier the UI renders truncated is unusable by construction, and no amount of calling the zome directly will reveal that** — the rehearsal supplies the hash from a variable. **WHAT IS NOT KNOWN:** whether any route exists for a non-developer. prediction about this**, which opens the form with the question bound and the claim's own words on screen. **The hash field is left out of the DOM entirely on that path, not hidden** — a field that is A bare hash would commit equally well but leaves the content nowhere."* But `SealedPrediction` appeared **twice** in the whole coordinator — constructed, then written — with **no read**. So `main.ts:4637` told the author *"Neither is recoverable from the DHT"* and made them retype the prediction and the salt. True of the DHT, where a private entry never goes; **false of the machine they are sitting at.** **WHY IT WAS THE WORST FAILURE THIS PRIMITIVE HAD:** the salt is unguessable *by design* (`lib.rs:803` — without it a prediction is brute-forceable from its hash), so an author who lost their note held a commitment that could **never** be revealed — permanently, publicly, on a chain with no delete, and counted in their own denominator as expired. **BUILT:** `get_my_sealed_prediction` — `query` with `include_entries(true)` over the caller's own chain, joined to the public half by **recomputing the commitment hash**, which is the same check validation performs at reveal. **`query` reaches only the calling agent's chain, so another agent's commitment is structurally unreachable rather than merely refused**; the explicit author check exists for the error message. **VERIFIED, NOT ASSUMED** — `pre-registration.mjs` §8, five checks on a live conductor, all green. **The check that carries it is the third: the reveal is driven ONLY by the recovered values**, nothing from the original commit, so a subtly lossy read would go red where matching strings would not. It is not positions. `validate_assessment` refuses any cone member that does not resolve to a **`Critique` of the same target**, so the set is the claim's OBJECTIONS and **there is no sibling-assessment cone anywhere in this protocol** — *did this assessor read the other assessments* is unanswerable by construction and no surface may imply otherwise. Both claims are struck in place rather than edited away. **SO THE NAME WAS THE FIRST DECISION, and it is not the one that was asked for.** The instruction said `assessments_unseen_by`. That name says the unseen things are assessments, which is exactly the wrong reading, and an extern name is permanent — it would have shipped the error into the API surface and sent a future reader looking for positions. Built as **`critiques_unseen_by_assessment`**, with SPEC §10 stating the asymmetry normatively rather than leaving the name pairing to imply a symmetry that does not exist. **WHAT THE QUERY ADDS OVER A CLIENT SUBTRACTING TWO LISTS, because a client can:** the gating rule travels with the data; the denominator is counted over THIS conductor's arc at read time rather than over whatever a panel happened to fetch; and `note` is the protocol's own statement of what the figures support, which a client would otherwise invent. That argument had to be made explicitly, since README §9 already warns against building the wrong caller to justify a call. **ONE BRANCH THE CRITIQUE SIDE NEVER NEEDED: a denominator of ZERO is the ORDINARY case here** — most claims carry no critiques — so *recorded nothing about what they had seen* would be true and would still mislead, inviting a reader to wonder what was missed when nothing existed. The screen, the SDK note and the MCP payload each say *there were no objections* instead. Nothing had checked it because until a second query of this shape existed there was no wrong hash to pass. | 6.10 | **The only cheap moment to improve a permanent entry is BEFORE it exists, and nothing is in that moment.** *Opened 2026-10-29 while answering "is AI already an ambient inhabitant, or are the hooks just in place?" — and it is the one AI use this repository's own stated reason against autonomous writing does not reach.* **MEASURED 2026-10-29 — AND THE FIRST MEASUREMENT WAS WRONG, CORRECTED THE SAME DAY.** `grep -rni 'anthropic\|openai\|claude-\|llm\|inference\|completion'` over `agent-sdk/src`, `mcp-server/src`, `mobile-ui/src` and `dna` returns **two hits, both incidental** — `main.ts:4179` quoting README §4.4, and `coordinator:3718` on HRR query completion. That is true of **the happ**, and the first version of this row generalised it to *no model exists anywhere in this project*, **which is false.** `notes/src/assistant.ts:193` calls `claude-opus-5` through the official SDK — tool-use structured output (`suggest_promotion`), `betas: ['server-side-fallback-2026-06-01']` with a refusal reported to the asker rather than swallowed, and a **dynamic** import so the notes server sharing the package never loads it. `notes/package.json` carries `@anthropic-ai/sdk ^0.124.0`. **The error was the sweep, not the reading: `notes/` was never in the grep**, because the dirs came from an earlier session's list rather than from `ls`. It is the defect [[sweep-adjacent-claims-not-just-the-one-you-came-for]] names, committed inside a row about measurement. **WHAT THE PRIOR ART ACTUALLY CHANGES, which is more than the correction.** (a) There is a working pattern in this repository to copy: credential-gated (`chooseSuggester` at `assistant.ts:269` falls back to fixed keyword rules when neither `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` nor `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` is set, **and says so**), dynamically imported, refusal-handled. (b) The notes assistant is this row's NEAREST NEIGHBOUR rather than a distant cousin — it already answers *"What should I know before publishing this?"* on a note below the promotion gate. **The distinction is narrower than first written**: the notes assistant advises on a NOTE in the soft off-DHT layer, where the next action is promotion; this row advises on a CLAIM DRAFT in the happ's own compose form, where the next action is a permanent DHT write under Invariant 6. The gate is what separates them, and that is the whole reason this row is about the happ's form specifically. (c) **IT ALSO PUTS THIS ROW IN TENSION WITH A CHOICE ALREADY MADE:** `notes/` uses the HOSTED Anthropic API, so *where inference runs* was decided once in this repository in the opposite direction from what §(3) below argues for. That is a real counter-argument rather than an oversight — the notes layer is off-DHT and its content was never promised to be local — and the decision in this row has to say why the happ's compose form is different rather than assume it. And the compose flow has nothing between the draft and the permanent write: the claim form's `onsubmit` (`main.ts:4582`) records evidence, builds the `Claim`, and calls `create_claim` (`main.ts:4624`) with no step in between. **WHY THIS IS THE FIRST AI ROW RATHER THAN THE INHABITANT.** README:1743 records why the swarm's dispositions are mechanical rather than generated — *"the reason is Invariant 6 rather than caution about language models: the first permanent writes an autonomous population ever makes should have a basis anybody can audit line by line."* A compose-time adversary **writes nothing**: no entry, no link, no §6 friction budget spent, no `protocol_version` bump, no DNA change, and nothing attributed to anybody's key. So that reason is silent here — and, unlike the inhabitant, it needs **no evaluation harness gating its output**, because the output is a suggestion on the author's own screen and the author is still the one who signs. That asymmetry is the whole argument for doing this one first. **WHAT IT WOULD DO:** generate the objections a claim will attract, before the claim exists, so the author can answer them, attach the evidence that is missing, or lower `confidence` while all three are still free. Distinct from a mode classifier — it attacks the claim rather than labelling it. **THREE HONESTY PROBLEMS, AND THE THIRD IS THE DECISION.** **(1)** Machine objections rendered near real ones would fabricate discourse: 6.1's rule applies unchanged and bites harder, because here one side is not a weaker promise but **nobody's promise at all**, and a screen that lets them sit at the same typographic weight invents critics. **(2)** A claim pre-hardened against a model's objections reads as better-grounded than it was, with nothing on the record saying a machine supplied the anticipation — arguably fine, since authors consult colleagues and cite nobody, but it should be decided rather than inherited; the related trap is an author raising `confidence` because the draft survived an adversary that observed nothing and published nothing. **(3) WHERE INFERENCE RUNS IS THE LOAD-BEARING CHOICE, and a hosted API would invert a privacy property this project has had for free so far.** The text of every draft — **including the claims the author decides NOT to publish** — would leave the machine before the entry exists. That is a disclosure channel the protocol has never had and structurally cannot govern, because nothing was committed for any rule to bite on, and it is worse than the published case rather than better: an abandoned draft is the one thing a user is entitled to assume stayed local. 7.19 already records that this repository discloses network metadata nowhere; this would add a second undisclosed flow, at compose time, to a third party. **Local-only inference is therefore the only version consistent with the membrane posture**, and two routes exist today: `yourownai.net` (built by Flowsta, AGPL, shipped on macOS/Windows/Linux, Holochain-backed signed transcripts) serves **an OpenAI-compatible endpoint on `localhost`**, so the call is a URL change with no key and no egress; Ollama is the generic fallback. **That makes the integration an ecosystem collaboration rather than a vendor pick** — a named Holochain-ecosystem dependency of the kind 7.21's door asks about, with a maintainer who already follows this project. **The boundary, restated because it is adjacent and easy to cross: this row puts a model on CLAIM DRAFTS and on nothing else.** 7.25 is already a decision about a false claim in public after `Pangram 95%`, and 7.24's article and 7.4's invitations stay in the author's own voice. **A SPIKE RAN ON 2026-10-29 AND IT MOVED BOTH DECISIONS, WITHOUT TAKING EITHER.** `scripts/adversary-spike.mjs` — writing nothing: no conductor, no entry, no link, no budget. Driven by **Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct Q4_K_M** through `llama-server` (the Vulkan build, offloaded to a GTX 970), which is **the same engine Your Own AI wraps**, so the route is the local one §(3) argues for and needed no hosted call. **FINDING 1 — THE SHAPE MUST BE CONSTRAINED AT THE SERVER, AND THIS REPOSITORY ALREADY KNEW IT.** Asked for one JSON object, the 3B model replied with a bare ARRAY of objections **and then a separate object** carrying `evidence_gap` and `confidence`. Spanning first `{` to last `}` swallows both and parses as neither, so a perfectly readable answer rendered as raw text. Fixed with `response_format: json_schema, strict` — supported by `llama-server`, with a single unconstrained retry for servers that lack it, and the render saying **UNCONSTRAINED** when that path was taken. This is `notes/src/assistant.ts`'s tool-use structured output arrived at a second time from the local side; the lenient parser survives as the fallback's safety net and now scans every top-level JSON value rather than one span. **FINDING 2, THE SUBSTANTIVE ONE — THE OBJECTIONS ARE USEFUL AND THE MODE LABELS ARE NOT.** Measured across runs: *Experiential* attached to *"can you provide personal or experiential accounts…"* and *Phenomenological* to *"the draft does not provide evidence of the specific inflammatory markers"*. Both are wrong in the same direction — **the model reads the five modes as TOPIC categories rather than as the kind of objection being made**, where `integrity/src/lib.rs:526-530` defines Experiential as the objector's *own* practice report and Phenomenological as *their* subjective experience diverging. The objection TEXT was consistently worth having (3-4 per draft, naming unmeasured variables and absent controls); the label on it was not. **WHY THAT DOES NOT SINK THIS ROW, AND WHAT IT IS EVIDENCE FOR INSTEAD.** Nothing here is published, so a wrong label is a defect on the author's own screen rather than a corrupted public statistic — which is exactly the asymmetry that put this row before the inhabitant. But it is **direct measured support for the claim that the inhabitant needs an evaluation harness**: `agent-sdk/examples/critique-agent.mjs`'s `decide()` contract warns that a dishonestly-chosen mode *"would corrupt the mode distribution that `get_discourse_health` reports, which is a real cost borne by everyone reading that domain"* — and a 3B model gets it wrong routinely, unprompted, on easy cases. The harness was the expensive half of that estimate; this is why. **AND IT DECIDES ONE SUB-QUESTION OF THE SECOND DECISION: `mode` MUST NOT BE AN ENUM IN THE SCHEMA.** Constraining it is trivially available and was deliberately NOT done — an enum forces a well-formed label at the sampler and thereby **hides** the misunderstanding, leaving the author five plausible-looking modes and no sign the vocabulary was missed. Shape errors are noise worth removing; mode errors are the finding. A free-string `mode` keeps the *NOT A MODE THIS PROTOCOL HAS* surfacing live for the unconstrained path. **A THIRD, SMALLER THING THE SCHEMA CANNOT FIX:** one constrained reply satisfied `required` by setting `evidence_gap` to the literal string `"..."`. A schema cannot forbid a well-formed string, so the render now drops a field whose content has no alphanumeric character — showing it would present a placeholder as a model's judgement. **WHAT THE SPIKE DID NOT SETTLE:** where inference runs for the SHIPPED feature is still open, because a spike on the author's own machine does not answer it for every installer; and how a machine objection renders beside a real one is untouched — the spike prints to a terminal, which is not a screen beside a critique panel. **THE ENDPOINT HALF OF THAT WAS CORRECTED BY THE MAINTAINER ON 2026-10-31, AND IT NARROWS THE DECISION RATHER THAN REOPENING IT.** The spike drove `llama-server` directly, and this row described that as *the same engine Your Own AI wraps*, so the route was the local one — **true of the engine, wrong as an integration**. Eric Doriean's answer: **do not build on the `llama-server` port.** It is an implementation detail — it restarts whenever the app switches models, its port may change, and **calls to it skip the person's AIs altogether**. The supported endpoint for another app on the same machine is **`http://localhost:11435/v1`**, OpenAI-compatible and documented (`docs.yourownai.net/external-apps/`, `/external-apps/connect`), up only while Your Own AI is open, with a health check at `/health`. **Three consequences land directly on this row's §(3), which is the privacy argument the whole route exists to serve.** (a) Two headers exist for exactly the unpublished-draft case this row is about: **`X-Your-Own-AI-Record: off`** keeps the exchange out of the person's records and **`X-Your-Own-AI-Memory: off`** leaves their memories out of the call — so the draft is neither retained by the app nor mixed with the person's own history. (b) **A calling app cannot force on-device answering.** Each call is routed by the chosen AI's own mode, so an AI set to an online model would send the draft off the machine — **the exact flow §(3) says the protocol cannot govern**. The integration must therefore **ask the person to select an offline-only AI** and say why, which is a UI obligation in this row rather than a configuration detail. (c) It is a **documented public interface**, not a private port — so the ecosystem-collaboration framing above strengthens rather than weakens. **Verified end-to-end here 2026-10-31**: `/v1/models` returns each AI twice (`<name>` and `<name>:agent`), both headers are accepted, and routing resolves — the call returns `503 routing_failed, "No offline models downloaded"` on this machine only because no model has been pulled through the GUI yet. **RUN AGAINST THE SUPPORTED ENDPOINT 2026-10-31, AND THE MODE FINDING REPRODUCED ON A DIFFERENT MODEL.** Ministral-3-3B-Instruct-2512-Q4_K_M downloaded into the app at the catalog's own pinned revision (exact byte match, 2,146,497,824 bytes), `scripts/adversary-spike.mjs` pointed at `localhost:11435/v1` with both privacy headers and `--model reeves`, three drafts, ~10s each. **Three things are new.** **(a) The endpoint honours `response_format: json_schema`** — every run came back constrained, so the *UNCONSTRAINED* fallback never fired and finding 1's server-side-shape remedy holds on this route too. **(b) The call is routed through the AI's persona, which is the whole difference from the raw port:** a six-word user message billed **385 prompt tokens**, and the reply came back tagged with the GGUF — the person's AI answered, not a bare model. **(c) THE MODE LABELS ARE WRONG IN THE SAME DIRECTION AS THE QWEN SPIKE, AND THE SPLIT IS SHARPER THAN FIRST RECORDED.** `Logical`, `Evidential` and `Methodological` were used correctly across all three drafts. `Experiential` and `Phenomenological` were wrong every time they appeared: on the zazen draft `Experiential` was attached to *what is the evidence that 'unconscious goal' is the primary cause... if this is anecdotal* — a demand for evidence rather than the objector's own practice report — and `Phenomenological` to a conceptual objection citing Zen's historical emphasis rather than a diverging subjective experience; on the trunk-based-development draft `Phenomenological` was attached to a textbook confounding objection. **The three modes that describe the STRUCTURE of an argument survive; the two defined by WHOSE experience grounds the objection do not** — `integrity/src/lib.rs:526-530` makes Experiential *I tried this and...* and Phenomenological *My subjective experience differs because...*, and both models read them as TOPIC categories instead. **That it reproduces on Ministral-3B after Qwen2.5-3B, through a different serving path and with a persona in front of it, makes it a property of small models reading this vocabulary rather than a quirk of one model** — which strengthens the no-enum decision and strengthens the evidence that the INHABITANT needs an eval harness. The objection TEXT remained worth having in all three runs, naming confounds, absent controls and unoperationalised terms. **AND THE MODE-LABEL FAILURE REPRODUCED A FOURTH TIME** on the verification run of that fix (Ministral 3 3B, persona mode, draft *"Every claim in this repo is counted"*): `Experiential` went to *are there substantive assumptions about the value or purpose of these claims collectively* — a structural question with no practice report in it — and `Phenomenological` to *what are the practical, lived, or theoretical consequences*, a consequences question rather than a diverging subjective experience. The three structural modes were again correct. **Four runs, two models, one serving path changed in between: this is settled, and the no-enum decision is safe.** **THE ENDPOINT HALF OF THE FIRST DECISION IS NOW CONSTRAINED BY A MEASUREMENT, 2026-11-01.** Your Own AI's maintainer, unprompted, named the variant an integration should use: `<ai-name>:agent`, or `X-Your-Own-AI-Mode: agent`. Reading `docs.yourownai.net/external-apps/connect` to confirm it turned up **a defect in this repository's own code**: the page says *"persona mode always includes memory"*, so `X-Your-Own-AI-Memory: off` **is honoured in agent mode ONLY**. All three callers here — `adversary-spike.mjs`, `generate-injections.mjs`, `check-prose-claims.mjs` — sent that header while taking `data[0].id`, which is the PERSONA variant (verified against the running app: `reeves, reeves:agent, teresa, ...`). **The header was a no-op and the author's own memories were being mixed into every call about a draft they might never publish** — the exact property the comment above it claimed. Fixed by preferring `:agent` and sending the mode header. **BUT AGENT MODE CANNOT RUN ON THIS MACHINE, AND THAT IS THE FINDING FOR THE SHIPPED FEATURE.** It requires an *agentic* model: Ministral 3 3B is refused (*"No installed model can drive agent work"*), and Qwen2.5-Coder-7B — which the directory scan DOES classify as agentic — is refused `MODEL_TOO_LARGE` on the 4 GB card, because the app will not split layers across GPU and CPU the way a hand-run `llama-server` does with `-ngl 20`. **So the privacy-preserving mode and the hardware that is actually here are mutually exclusive today**, and the scripts now REFUSE rather than fall back to the leak; `YOAI_ALLOW_PERSONA=1` is the author's explicit consent. For the shipped feature this rules out one answer to *where inference runs*: routing through a person's own AI is the right shape ethically and **is not available on a low-VRAM machine**, so either the feature carries its own model, or it must degrade in a way that states the privacy cost out loud rather than silently sending memories. **ERIC ANSWERED ON 2026-11-03, AND ONE OF THIS ROW'S LOAD-BEARING CONSTRAINTS HAS AN EXPIRY DATE.** **(1) `X-Your-Own-AI-Memory: off` WILL WORK WITH THE PLAIN MODEL** in the next Your Own AI release — send the header and the AI answers as itself, without its memories, with no agent mode required. So *memory-off is agent-mode only*, which is **why the YOAI route was ruled refuse-or-leak on this card**, stops being true when that ships. **(2) SPLIT OFFLOAD FOR AGENT MODE IS A DELIBERATE LINE, with reasons sharper than the question assumed:** an agent session runs its model for many steps in a row and **the agent instructions alone are ~9,000 tokens**, so a model that *only just fits* is where they measured agent sessions turning into timeouts on small cards; the engine's automatic splitting also **crashed on hard card-and-CPU splits**, so the app leaves it off. A hand-run `-ngl 20` is fine for chat and is a much lighter load than a long prompt on every step. An opt-in split for dense models, marked slower, is something he is considering. **(3) AND A ROUTE THIS ROW DID NOT KNOW ABOUT: mixture-of-experts agentic models run with their EXPERTS IN MAIN MEMORY and the rest on the card**, badged *GPU + RAM* on the Offline Models page, gated on RAM and CPU rather than VRAM. **Granite 4.0 Tiny and LFM2.5** are the small ones. **So *agent mode cannot run on this machine* was true of DENSE models and is not true in general** — a correction to this row's own claim. **WHAT THIS DOES AND DOES NOT DO TO THE DECISION. It does NOT reverse it.** Carrying a model was decided on four grounds — universal, fully local, independent of a second app, no routing UI obligation — and **availability was only one of them.** The other three stand, and **the load-bearing one is untouched: a calling app still cannot force on-device answering**, so an AI set to an online model would send the draft off the machine, which is exactly the flow §(3) says the protocol cannot govern. **WHAT IT OPENS is the option rejected partly on availability:** prefer the person's own AI when it is present and answering locally, fall back to the carried model. **Worth revisiting once that release lands and not before** — the feature works today and nothing is blocked on it. **AND (3) IS NOW MEASURED, 2026-11-03 — THE GPU + RAM ROUTE REALLY DOES CARRY AN AGENT LOOP ON THIS 4 GB CARD, BUT ONLY FOR ONE OF THE TWO MODELS HE NAMED.** Run through Eric's OWN shipped engine and headless matrix (`cargo test --lib -- --ignored live_matrix_* --nocapture` in `~/Your-Own-AI/src-tauri`, head of `main` `6935069`), which is the point of the exercise: the numbers come from his decision path, not an approximation of it. **LFM2.5-8B-A1B PASSES EVERY GATE:** auto-pick `--n-cpu-moe 18` of 24 layers, load 39.8 s, **123 prompt / 39.9 gen tok/s**, compact-cache arm within 5% — against `agent_caps("lfm2")` **6** >= 6, `agent_template_ok` true (`tools` + `tool_calls`, no `raise_exception`+`alternate`), `fit.is_fast()` **Split** (earned on real figures: `offload_wanted(5.78 > 3.56)`, RAM budget 11.3 GB, `offload_fits(4.80 <= 11.3, 5.78 <= 14.86)`, active fraction **0.125** — 4 of 32 experts, no shared expert — under the 0.30 ceiling), and `measured_tps` **39.9** >= `AGENT_MIN_TPS` 8.0. **GRANITE 4.0 TINY CANNOT, AND NOT FOR A HARDWARE REASON:** `agent_caps` returns **5** for `granite-4` against the `>= 6` filter at `router.rs:893`, with no runtime promotion path — the rule above `agent_caps` promotes a family only after a live folder test, i.e. a maintainer source change. So the model he named first is excluded by his own code on every machine, and this row must not report it as a 4 GB option. **TWO MEASUREMENT TRAPS, both of which caught the first reading and are the reusable part:** `leg_fit_truth` called with `app: None` prints the **plain dense** grade and not `assess()`'s, so it says `claimed RED, actually RAN` for a model the app grades Split — **a headless Red is an artifact, not the app's verdict**, and reporting it as one would have inverted the finding; and the dense estimator is pessimistic by **2.8x** here (5.78 GB predicted, **2.05 GB** actual), which is what produces that Red. Separately `live_matrix_tune_bench` hardcodes `total_ram_gb = 31.0` into `arms_for` -> `choose_ctx`, so a small-machine run need not bench the app's own context choice — latent, and it did NOT change this run (both legs chose ctx 32768 regardless), so it is reported as a harness defect and not as a result. **STILL UNMEASURED: gen tok/s at ~9k depth**, the figure his timeout observation is really about — it cannot change the gate, because the app's own `measured_tps` IS the tune bench's near-empty-context `gen_tps`. **WHAT IT CHANGES HERE: the refuse-rather-than-leak scripts now have a local path that answers**, since `LFM2.5-8B-A1B-Q4_K_M.gguf` is in the models dir; Granite's download was interrupted at 2.36 of 4.2 GB and is not worth finishing.  **THE DECISIONS, TAKEN 2026-11-02, ALL FOUR, AND THE SHAPE THEY MAKE IS SIMPLER THAN THE DEBATE WAS.** **(1) WHERE INFERENCE RUNS: THE FEATURE CARRIES ITS OWN MODEL.** Not the person's Your Own AI, and not a hosted API. The YOAI route was the right SHAPE and lost on availability: `X-Your-Own-AI-Memory: off` is honoured in agent mode only, agent mode needs an agentic model that fits, and that excludes the hardware the invited cohort actually has — so that route is *refuse* or *leak* on a modest machine, and a feature most installers never see is not a feature. Carrying a model wins on four counts the debate had been treating as separate: it is **universal** (every installer gets it), **fully local** (no egress on any path, so §(3)'s disclosure channel never opens), **independent** (no second app need be running), and it **removes a UI obligation** — there is no *please select an offline-only AI, and here is why* screen, because there is no routing for the person to get wrong. The spike is what makes this affordable rather than a compromise: four runs across two models and two serving paths established that the objection TEXT is consistently worth having at the 3B class, and that the only thing a small model reliably gets wrong is the mode label — **which this feature does not use.** The quality objection to a carried model lands on the half being discarded. **WHAT THIS DECISION DOES NOT SETTLE, and it is an implementation question rather than a `[D]`:** whether the model is bundled into the desktop build or pulled on first use. Bundling makes the download large and the feature instant; pulling keeps the installer small and makes the first use a wait with a progress bar and a refusal path. Decide it against the release artifact, not here. **(2) HOW A MACHINE OBJECTION RENDERS BESIDE A REAL ONE: IT NEVER DOES.** Compose-time only. The objections exist in the draft form, before the claim exists, and are gone the moment it is published; the critique panel never shows one, and no surface co-locates them. **This dissolves the honesty problem rather than answering it** — §(1) worried that machine objections at the same typographic weight as attributed ones would invent critics, and 6.1 spent weeks establishing how to render two unequal claims honestly side by side. The lesson 6.1 actually earned is that **sometimes the honest answer to *how do we show this beside that* is *not beside it*.** There is no shared surface, so there is no weight to equalise, no caption to get right, and no injection that can make a machine look like a critic. **(3) NOTHING GOES ON THE RECORD.** No field, no link, no marker that a model reviewed the draft. Authors consult colleagues, reviewers and textbooks and cite none of them; a compose-time adversary writes nothing, is attributed to nobody, and the author is still the one who signs. A flag would make this one kind of consultation uniquely visible among all the kinds an author has, and under Invariant 6 it would be permanent and cost an integrity change and a fork. **§(2)'s trap is real and is answered on the SCREEN rather than in the record** — the form must say that surviving these objections is not evidence, because the model observed nothing and published nothing, so it cannot license a higher `confidence`. **BUILT 2026-11-02, AND THE SCOPE IS SPLIT ACROSS TWO REPOSITORIES, which this row did not say.** `mobile-ui/src/adversary.ts` (the endpoint client, the schema, the lenient parser, the placeholder drop) and the compose form's *Before you sign* section; `scripts/live-verify/compose-adversary.mjs`, **30 checks**, in `ui.yml`. **WHAT IS NOT DONE: pulling and serving the weights.** Decided 2026-11-02 to **pull on first use rather than bundle** — the desktop installers are **113-150MB** today and a 3B Q4_K_M is **~2.1GB**, so bundling would make each of six platform artifacts about fifteen times larger. **That half lives in `StateofIntent/epistemic-resonance-desktop`**, a separate repository holding electron-builder, auto-update and the `breakingVersion` mapping. ~~The interaction with `breakingVersion` is unexamined~~ **THAT WAS WRONG AND IS WITHDRAWN, checked 2026-11-02: it is examined thoroughly, in that repo's own `kangaroo.config.ts`**, which records *"A MINOR HERE WOULD HAVE FORKED THE NETWORK AND ABANDONED THE DATA ON BOTH MACHINES THAT RAN 0.3.0"* and proves the `v0.3.1` patch by hashing both webhapps' DNAs rather than reading the diff. **The rule is established; what the model work adds is a tension it does not anticipate.** **THE MECHANICS, READ RATHER THAN RECALLED.** `breakingVersion` maps `0.<minor>.<patch>` to the literal `0.<minor>.x`, and that string has THREE consumers, not two: `defaultAppNetworkSeed()` in `cli.ts` makes it the network-seed suffix (so a change forks the network); `KangarooFileSystem.connect` makes it a path segment for `userData`, `logs` and `sessionData` (so a change gives a fresh conductor — **new keystore and new agent key**, not merely abandoned data); and `index.ts:382` gates auto-update on `breakingVersion(new) === breakingVersion(current)`. **THAT THIRD CONSUMER CHANGES WHAT THE HAZARD IS.** A minor would not silently move anybody: electron-updater would REFUSE to offer it, so existing installs would sit on `0.3.x` and never be told a newer version exists. **Stranded rather than forked** — milder, still bad, and invisible. **SO THE MODEL MUST SHIP AS A PATCH (`0.3.2`), WHICH IS THE OPPOSITE OF WHAT CONVENTION SAYS.** Adding a feature is a minor everywhere else — it is what the npm packages did today going to `0.3.0` — and here a minor at `0.x` is a **network migration event**. **A patch is not a loophole for this change, because the model is app-side and the DNA hash is untouched**: `kangaroo.config.ts`'s own test, hash both webhapps and confirm identical, passes trivially when no zome changed. And `installHappIfNecessary`'s early return — the thing that kept 1.18 from reaching upgraded installs — does not bite here for the same reason: nothing in the happ changes. **THAT A WEBVIEW MAY CALL A LOCALHOST SERVICE WAS VERIFIED RATHER THAN ASSUMED** — `notes.ts` already fetches `http://localhost:8790` in a feature that ships, so the pattern was copied. **THE ORIGIN CHECK IS IN THE CLIENT, NOT THE SCREEN:** `isLocalOrigin` refuses any non-localhost endpoint, because §(3)'s privacy property would otherwise be given away through a settings box, and a refusal in a screen can be bypassed where one in the client cannot. **TWO DEFECTS FOUND BY BUILDING IT, BOTH THE SAME SHAPE — STATE CHANGED WITHOUT A RENDER, which is 1.23's invariant from the other side.** (1) The run button's `disabled` is computed when the form is built and typing deliberately does not rebuild, so an author typed a draft and the button stayed off until something unrelated re-rendered; the content field now toggles it directly. (2) `clearAfterPublish` nulled the verdict and **the objections stayed on screen**, because the publish path avoids `render()` on purpose — a rebuild there would let `captureFieldValues` restore the text the publish just cleared. The verdict's DOM now lives in one container that path empties. **The second defect was 6.10's own second decision broken by an invisible gap between state and DOM.** **INJECTIONS DA-DC, AND DA IS THE RESULT WORTH KEEPING: IT WENT GREEN.** §3 asserted only the COUNT of unreal-mode markers — one marker, fewer markers than objections — and **both stay true when the marking is exactly inverted**, so the section passed against a client that marked the real mode and spared the invented one. **A count cannot tell you which row it is on.** Fixed by reading the marker's own row. DB (placeholder rendered) 1 red; DC (verdict survives the publish) 2 red with the publish control green. **And the DC run was wrong the first time** because the injected file did not type-check, the build failed, and the harness ran the PREVIOUS injection's bundle with the build output piped to `/dev/null`. **Next free injection ID: DD.** **THE RUNTIME IS PINNED, 2026-11-02 — llama.cpp, MIT, Vulkan where it exists** (desktop #13). The AGPL alternative was Your Own AI's own build output, which is a licensing question before an engineering one. **The Vulkan archives carry the per-microarchitecture CPU backends beside `libggml-vulkan.so`, so a machine with no Vulkan driver falls back to CPU rather than failing to start** — read out of the archive rather than assumed. 11-36MB per target against a 113-150MB installer, which is why the RUNTIME is bundled and the WEIGHTS are pulled. **AND VERIFYING IT PRODUCED A FIFTH REPRODUCTION OF THE MODE FAILURE PLUS A NEW ONE THE SCHEMA CANNOT STOP.** Serving `qwen2.5-3b-instruct-q4_k_m` through the pinned build and sending the adversary's exact constrained request came back with **seven** objections, of which **two were not objections at all**: entries with `mode: "evidence_gap"` and `mode: "confidence"`, carrying prose that belonged in the top-level fields — **which the model ALSO filled**, putting `"High"` in `evidence_gap` where a description of the missing evidence was asked for. **SO A STRICT `json_schema` DOES NOT STOP A MODEL USING THE OBJECTIONS ARRAY AS A DUMPING GROUND FOR THE OTHER FIELDS.** `strict: true` and `additionalProperties: false` were both honoured — every entry had exactly `mode` and `text` — and the shape was still wrong in a way no schema can express, because the error is about WHICH THINGS ARE OBJECTIONS rather than about structure. **The client handles it correctly and by accident of a good decision:** `modeIsReal` marks both as *NOT A MODE THIS PROTOCOL HAS*, so they are surfaced rather than rendered as objections with odd labels — which is the free-string `mode` earning its keep a second time, for a failure it was not designed for. **WHAT IS NOT HANDLED, AND IS LEFT ALONE DELIBERATELY:** `evidence_gap: "High"` passes `meaningful()` and renders as *"What would answer the strongest of these: High"*, which is useless but is the model's own output. The client states figures and surfaces labels; it does not adjudicate content, and a filter that dropped answers it judged unhelpful would be the client deciding what the author may read. **(4) `mode` STAYS A FREE STRING** — decided earlier by measurement, restated here because the three decisions above are consistent with it: the feature shows objection text and never a mode, so the enum question is moot for the shipped form and the free string keeps *NOT A MODE THIS PROTOCOL HAS* visible in the spike. | ~~**D** twice~~ ~~**ONE AND A HALF LEFT**~~ ~~**ALL DECISIONS TAKEN**~~ **BUILT 2026-11-02 — the client half. | 6.12 | **A reader who is told a domain name can read it; a reader who is not told has no way to find it. Nothing in the app or the protocol enumerates the domains that hold claims.** *Opened 2026-11-09 by a pilot participant's second message after publishing — "how do you search for what others wrote?"* The first time that question has been asked by somebody who was not told the answer in advance, and it has no good answer. **WHAT EXISTS: three discovery reads, and every one takes something the reader must already hold.** `get_claims_by_domain(domain)` hashes `Path::from(format!("domain_{}", domain))` (`dna/coordinator/src/lib.rs:5136`), so the lookup is a literal string match and `mental health reform` and `Mental Health Reform` are different domains that cannot find each other; `get_claims_by_agent` needs a base64 agent key; `get_membranes` needs nothing and returns only the domains somebody FOUNDED. **There is no `get_domains` and no `list_domains` in either zome** — checked against the full extern list, not inferred. **AND FOUNDING IS NOT REQUIRED TO PUBLISH** — 1.4 established that by measurement, `validate_claim` wants only a non-empty `domain` — so the one read that needs no prior knowledge is structurally incapable of listing the domains most likely to be missing from it. **THE BREADCRUMB WAS MISSING TOO, and that was the cheap half — BUILT 2026-11-10, see the end of this row.** As written, `renderClaimCard` printed content, confidence and author and **not the claim's own domain**; the only place `entry.domain` reached a screen anywhere in `mobile-ui/src/main.ts` was the notes room's target picker at line 549. **ONE NEAR-MISS, FOUND BY A PARTICIPANT RATHER THAN BY THIS ROW, AND IT SHARPENS THE POINT RATHER THAN SOFTENING IT:** the Worldline tab's period list DOES render a domain string, as `` `${start_time} · ${domain_tag}` `` at line 3753 — it is how Kupukupu quoted `2026-11-09 08:14 · Mental Health Reform` back on the day they published. But that is `WorldlineBoundary.domain_tag` on the reader's OWN trace, not `Claim.domain` on anybody's claim, **so the one screen in the app that shows you a domain shows you only the domains you already wrote in.** A reader can learn their own strings and never anyone else's, which is the row's thesis stated by the one counterexample to it. So a reader who arrives at one claim by any route cannot learn the string that would show them its neighbours, and the By Author byline — the one affordance built for exactly this move — carries them to an agent rather than to a field. **AND BROWSE CANNOT SAY A DOMAIN IS EMPTY — that is 6.13**, split out of this row on 2026-11-09 because it is a defect in its own right and bites every domain read rather than only the ones a reader could not find. It compounded this one: a reader who guessed the string wrongly and a reader who guessed it rightly into an empty domain were shown **the same sentence**, so the app could not tell them which of the two problems they had. **That is closed as of 2026-11-10, and the fix carries this row's reasoning rather than only its own** — the empty answer now names the typo as the other hypothesis, because this row is why a reader cannot rule it out. **WHY THE ENUMERATION HALF IS `[D]` AND NOT WIRING.** `SECURITY.md`'s honest half, quoted by 7.12 and by the coordinator above `get_membrane_claims`: **containment holds for what you traverse to and nothing for what you look up.** A critique-graph walk degrades under a flood; an anchor index returns the flood in full. The project already decided the fix is a read-layer lens, and anchored it to a MEMBRANE rather than a bare domain string precisely so that a caller cannot ask about a domain nobody founded and so the membrane-scoped `AttestationGrant` has something to be scoped by. **An index of every domain string anybody ever typed is the one shape that decision forecloses** — a bare-string enumeration of the whole network with nothing for a lens to attach to. So `get_membranes` may already BE the answer, and the real defect is that no screen says so: a reader is left to infer that the Domains tab is the index when it is a registry of the founded. **THE SPLIT, AND A PARTICIPANT CUT IT BETTER THAN THIS ROW DID — SEE 6.16, WHICH IS THE HALF THAT IS NOT BLOCKED ON ANYTHING HERE.** *2026-11-09, unprompted:* getting BACK to a domain is a different problem from finding one you have never seen, and **only the second carries this row's `[D]`**. That half was written into this row first and then split out on the same day, because an unblocked `[M]` living inside a row whose last column reads *blocked on a decision* is invisible to the scan this file tells a reader to run — the defect `check-row-status.mjs` exists to catch, in a different costume. The breadcrumb is `[M]` and small — render the domain on the claim card; the empty state is 6.13. The enumeration is `[D]`: either founding becomes the price of being findable (which makes the Domains tab honest and gives the lens its anchor, at the cost of a step before a first claim), or a second uncontained index gets built against the reasoning that removed the last one. **AND THE FIRST OUTSIDE EVIDENCE ON THAT `[D]` ARRIVED 2026-11-09, FROM THE PERSON WHO WOULD PAY THE COST.** Kupukupu — a self-described non-technical user, and the pilot's first publisher — was given the honest version of the lookup-versus-traversal argument and answered: *"requiring founding to be findable seems fair, as long as it's simple and the app explains why when you create a domain"*, with the risk named back — *"interesting topics in unfounded domains could exist but never be found by others"* — and a mechanism proposed: **prompt people to found a domain at the moment they create one**, so good topics are not lost by accident. **That is the evidence this row could not generate for itself**, because the objection to charging a step before a first claim is entirely about what it costs a newcomer, and the only people who can price it are newcomers. It is one person and should not be read as more; it is also the first time anyone outside this repository has been asked. **THE MECHANISM IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING EVEN IF THE `[D]` GOES THE OTHER WAY** — a prompt at creation time is not a gate, so it costs nothing in the branch where founding stays optional, and it converts the choice from one nobody knew they were making into one they declined. **AND THEN THEY DID THE OPPOSITE OF WHAT THEY SAID, WHICH IS THE BETTER EVIDENCE.** Hours later they published the position as a claim — *"Asking people to found a domain before others can find it is worth the extra step. A topic nobody can find is worth less than one that's just one more click away"* — and filed it **into somebody else's existing domain rather than founding one of their own**, because that domain was the one place guaranteed to be read. **That is not hypocrisy, it is the rational move, and it is exactly what this row predicts**: offered a choice between topical precision and being found, a participant picks being found. A stated preference for the founding step and a revealed preference against paying it are both evidence, and **the revealed one is the one a design should be built against** — it says the cost is real even to somebody who has just finished arguing it is worth paying. It also means the claim arguing for findability would itself have been unfindable had they followed their own advice, which is the row's thesis demonstrated twice in one act. **THE OPERATIONAL COST IS ALREADY BEING PAID.** The pilot's domain strings are passed around by hand in Telegram; one wrong domain has already been sent to a participant and corrected after the fact (`pilot-state-2026-11-08`); and a typo makes a second domain with one claim in it, with no rename and no way for anyone to find either. **THE `[M]` HALF IS BUILT, 2026-11-10, AND THE `[D]` HALF IS UNTOUCHED BY IT.** `renderClaimCard` now prints the claim's domain beside its author, as a button that loads that domain in Browse — routed through `loadClaims` with `fromTheBox` false, which is the case that function's own comment already described as *following a domain from a card* before any card offered one. **It is asserted from the By Author tab rather than from Browse, and that distinction is the whole value of the check**: on Browse the box's domain and the claim's domain are the same string, so a breadcrumb rendered from `currentDomain` would pass there by coincidence. Injected exactly that, and it went **4 red on By Author while the check COUNTING breadcrumbs stayed GREEN** — two cards naming the same wrong domain are still two cards naming a domain. `browse-states.mjs`, phase 5. **WHAT THIS DOES NOT DO, said plainly because the row's value is the split:** it gives a reader who has arrived at one claim the string for its neighbours, and it gives a reader who has arrived nowhere nothing at all. The enumeration question — whether founding becomes the price of being findable — is where it was, with one participant's stated preference for the step and the same participant's revealed preference against paying it as the only outside evidence on file. | ~~**M** for the card~~ **the card is BUILT 2026-11-10**, **D** for the index | ~~nothing for the `[M]` half~~ **`[M]` done**; the `[D]` half needs the founding-as-the-price-of-findability question answered |
| 6.13 | ~~**The Browse tab has one state where it needs four, so a reader cannot tell a domain that is empty from one they never loaded — or from a read that failed.**~~ **BUILT 2026-11-10, and the row's own prediction about the unhandled rejection was confirmed by measurement rather than left as reasoning.** *Split out of 6.12 on 2026-11-09, having been found by a verification run rather than by reading the code.* `renderBrowseTab` prints exactly one sentence whenever `claims` is empty: `No claims loaded yet for "X"` once a domain has ever been set, and *Enter a domain above and load its claims* before that. `loadClaims` (`mobile-ui/src/main.ts:2818`) sets `currentDomain` and then awaits the zome call with **no loading state and no loaded-empty state**, so the sentence shown after a completed read of an empty domain is byte-for-byte the sentence shown before any read ran. **AND THERE IS NO ERROR STATE EITHER.** `loadClaims` has no `try`, and its call site — `loadBtn.onclick = () => loadClaims(domainInput.value.trim(), true)` — neither awaits nor catches, so a rejected zome call becomes an unhandled rejection and the screen keeps whatever it was already showing. A conductor that is down, a malformed domain, and a domain with nothing in it are **one indistinguishable outcome**. It also marks the onboarding step `browsed-domain` done *before* the await, so a read that never succeeded still advances the tour. **THE SAME FILE ALREADY SOLVES THIS, DELIBERATELY AND WITH ITS REASONS WRITTEN DOWN.** `renderByAuthorTab` branches four ways — `authorError` for a failure, *Enter an agent key above, or click an author on any claim.* for not-yet-asked, `Reading…` while `!authorLoaded`, and `This agent has published no claims.` with `data-testid="author-empty"` for a real empty answer, carrying the comment *"A real answer, and a different one from 'not asked yet'."* Browse has the identical need, none of the distinction, and **no testid on its empty case at all**, so no harness can assert the difference and no gate can catch a regression in it. **HOW IT WAS FOUND, which is the part that makes it more than a polish item.** On 2026-11-09 the shipped `v0.3.3` desktop build was driven to answer whether a pilot participant's claims had landed. `Mental Health Reform` returned zero, and **the screen could not say whether the read had happened** — it took loading a second, known-populated domain (`xnet-1026-a`, which rendered its claim) as a positive control to establish that the button worked at all. A verification that needs a control to interpret its own UI is the defect stated as an experiment. **AND 7.29 MAKES THE EMPTY CASE THE COMMON ONE RATHER THAN THE RARE ONE.** With no always-on node, a correct read of a domain that genuinely holds claims returns zero whenever their author is offline — so *zero* is now the answer a participant will see most often, and it is precisely the answer this screen refuses to characterise. **THE FIX IS THE EXISTING PATTERN APPLIED**, not a design question: a `browseLoaded` flag and a `browseError` string alongside the ones By Author already has, the four sentences it already writes, and a testid on the empty case so a harness can hold it. **WHAT WAS BUILT, 2026-11-10 — exactly that, plus two things the row had not asked for and one it had.** The four states are By Author's, copied rather than invented: `browse-error` returns early, `Reading…` shows while `browseLoaded` is false, and `browse-empty` carries a real answer. **The empty sentence names the likely cause rather than only the fact** — *nobody has published here, or the domain string is not the one you were given; A file containing only those would have certified the UI being replaced. `markDone` moved back above the await: **exactly 1 red**, invisible to every check that reads the screen. **AND THE HARNESS HAD TO BE HARDENED BEFORE IT COULD COUNT ANYTHING**: its first run against the pre-fix screen DIED at `locator.inputValue: Timeout 30000ms exceeded` on an element that screen never rendered. | 6.19 | **The protocol lets a critique target another critique and this app only ever targets a Claim — so the defence we told the pilot's first publisher was available cannot be reached from the build in their hands.** *Opened 2026-11-11 by the first half of the same sentence — "It doesnt show if an author responded" — which 6.18's byline answers for WHO and does not answer for WHAT A ROW REPLIES TO.* **MEASURED, AND THE GAP IS ENTIRELY CLIENT-SIDE.** `CritiqueTargetType` covers Claim, Critique, Constitution, Membrane and CritiqueSpecies (`dna/integrity/src/lib.rs:149`); `validate_critique`'s only author rule is that `critique.author` matches the action author, which is anti-spoofing and **not** anti-self; `get_critiques_for` takes an `AnyDhtHash` and `loadCritiqueTree` ALREADY walks it recursively to depth 3, so the READ of a reply exists and is bounded; and `agent-sdk/src/index.ts:859` accepts a `targetType` with `'Claim'` only as its default, so a scripted agent can already do this. The flat list is in the order the DHT returned it and a nested reply has no place in that order; indenting it is a client deciding a thread's shape, and 6.14's `[D]` on entry order is the same question one row over. **THE `[D]` IS ANSWERED, AND IT WAS ANSWERED BY THE PERSON THE ROW IS OWED TO — 2026-11-12, UNASKED AND WITHOUT HAVING SEEN THIS ROW.** Replying about the release, they wrote: *"I think the most logical way to go about this would be that the reply comes under a critique, in where others agreeing or disagreeing with that particular critique to the claim can reply. A new critique gets its own section. It provides organisation."* **WHAT THAT SETTLES, PRECISELY.** A reply renders **under the critique it targets**, and **each top-level critique becomes its own section** rather than one more line in a flat list. The DHT's return order still governs the order of the sections; what it never had to govern is where a reply sits, because **a reply's place is given by its target rather than by its arrival** — which is the thing this row could not see from the inside. **WHY THIS COUNTS AS THE DECISION AND NOT AS A PREFERENCE.** The objection was never that indentation is ugly; it was that indenting is *a client deciding a thread's shape* on behalf of readers who did not ask for it. That objection is not answered by reasoning and cannot be — it is answered by a reader asking for it, which is what happened, from the only participant who has both published a claim and been critiqued on it. **The decision was held for a human and a human made it; it was simply not the human this file was waiting for.** **IT DOES NOT ANSWER 6.14's `[D]`**, and the distinction is exactly the one above: 6.14 is the order of entries with no target to hang from, where arrival order is all there is. It is now the protocol's own five-variant union, and `AntibodyPattern` two hundred lines below had spelled those five out all along. **THE FORM IS EXTRACTED RATHER THAN COPIED, and the reason is the friction gate rather than tidiness:** a reply IS a critique, `create_critique` creates a SynapticLink either way, and that is the one coordinator path spending the SWO budget — so a hand-rolled reply form would have been an affordance that bypassed a protocol limit the main form honours, offering an action this client already knows will be refused. One `buildCritiqueForm`, one gate, two targets. **THE READ IS ONE LEVEL AND DELIBERATELY NOT THE TREE.** `loadCritiqueTree` already walks to depth 3, but the note above *View critique structure* records it as opt-in per claim because it is a recursive fan-out — and **a reply nobody sees without opening a spatial view is a reply that does not exist for readers.** So `loadReplies` is one `get_critiques_for` per critique already on screen, settled per critique the way `loadMyCritiqueCounts` is: one rejected read leaves that critique absent from the map rather than failing the batch, because **an unanswered critique is a claim about the discourse and not one this client should make by accident.** Unasked renders as *Checking for replies…*, which is 6.13's distinction applied where it bites hardest. **WHAT A REPLY DELIBERATELY DOES NOT CARRY:** the cone line, the unseen denominator and the conductance, all three of which are computed against the CLAIM's critique set — `renderUnseenState` takes that list as its siblings and `conductanceByCritique` is keyed off links loaded for it. Printing them on a reply would attach a denominator that was never measured over replies: a figure that looks like the one above it and means something else. **IT DOES CARRY WHO IS SPEAKING, with a third relation a critique cannot have — *the critique's author* — because a reply sits between two authors rather than beside one.** **THE INDENT IS A BORDER, NOT A MARGIN**, since `layout-fits.mjs` refuses sideways scroll at 390px and a left border says *attached to the thing above* in about 12px. **GATED IN `reading-a-thread.mjs`, now 47 checks (was 30), with the placement check carrying three controls because a reply that renders SOMEWHERE is the easy half.** The write is measured through the real form, and **validation does the hard part for us: `validate_critique` reads the target entry back and cross-checks its REAL type against the declared `target_type` (`dna/integrity/src/lib.rs:1719-1734`), refusing a mismatch — so a reply that commits at all is proof the client sent `'Critique'`.** **THREE INJECTIONS, ALL RED IN THE RIGHT PLACE.** Appending the reply at panel level — the shape this row argued against — **3 red**.
